]
.

4.3
Generalization to the spin case

In this section, we briefly mention the relation between
\widehat{Z}
and the spin version of the
\mathrm{N}_{r}
invariant for
r=0\bmod 4
which was defined in
[
11
]
. We will omit any details and intermediate calculations, as they are analogous to the non-spin case. The spin version of the invariant depends on the choice of
\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
-spin structure, which can be understood as a homotopy class of lifts

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Spin}(3,\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\operatorname{SO}(3),}

(4.9)

where
\mathrm{Spin}(3,\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
is an extension of
\operatorname{SO}(3)
by
\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
equipped with discrete topology:

(\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})_{\text{discrete}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spin}(3,\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})\equiv\mathrm{Spin}(3)\times_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})_{\text{discrete}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}(3),

the vertical map in (
4.9
) corresponds to the projection, and the horizontal map is the classifying map of the bundle of orthonormal frames in the tangent bundle,
which are non-canonically parametrized by
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
. To formulate the relation, it will be useful to consider the canonical map

\tilde{\sigma}\colon\ H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})\times\mathrm{Spin}(M)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spin}(M,\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})

similar to the map
\sigma
. It is induced by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
quotient map of the product
\mathrm{Spin}(3)\times(\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})_{\text{discrete}}
, taking into account that
B(\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})_{\text{discrete}}=K(\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z},1)
. Then, for general
b_{1}
the relation for
r=0\bmod 4
reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}N^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{r}(M,\tilde{\sigma}(\omega,s))={}&\frac{(-1)^{\mu(M,s)}r^{b_{1}}\mathcal{T}([\omega])}{\sqrt{|\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}}\\
&\times\!\!\!\lim_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b\in\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})\\
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{b_{1}}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}{\ell k}(a,a)-2\pi{\rm i}{\ell k}(a,b)-\pi{\rm i}\omega(a)+\pi{\rm i}\omega^{\prime\prime}(m)}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(b\oplus\frac{mr}{2},s)}(M),\end{split}\!\!\!

where
\mu(M,s)
is the Rokhlin invariant, for which we used the surgery formula (
2.17
). Note that modulo two it is actually independent of spin structure
s
.
For rational homology spheres, the relation simplifies to

\displaystyle N^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{r}(M,\tilde{\sigma}(\omega,s))

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{(-1)^{\mu(M,s)}\mathcal{T}([\omega])}{\sqrt{|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}}\lim_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}\sum_{{a,b\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}{\ell k}(a,a)-2\pi{\rm i}{\ell k}(a,b)-\pi{\rm i}\omega(a)}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(b,s)}(M).

5
\bm{\widehat{Z}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{N}_{r}}
as decorated TQFTs

Both
\widehat{Z}
and
\mathrm{N}_{r}
are examples of topological invariants that, on the one hand, behave well under cutting and gluing (admit surgery formulae) and, on the other hand, depend on additional data (decoration).
In this section, which is of more speculative nature, we describe how this additional data should behave under cutting and gluing as well as its transformation under the operation of taking the limit
q\to{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}
that conjecturally relates
\widehat{Z}
and
\mathrm{N}_{r}
invariants.

5.1
Hilbert space on a torus

In table (
1.1
) we omitted the comparison of Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{BCGP}}\big(T^{2}\big)
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{GPPV}}\big(T^{2}\big)
in the two theories on a 2-torus.
15
15

15

BCGP stands for the TQFT built out of the CGP invariants by Blanchet, Costantino, Geer and Patureau
[
12
]
.
These spaces control surgery operations and deserve a separate section. Note, in a semisimple TQFT with a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, we have

{\mathcal{H}}\big(T^{2}\big)=K^{0}\big(\text{MTC}\big(S^{1}\big)\big).

Neither of the two theories we are trying to compare fits into this standard paradigm of the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction, which nevertheless can be used as a good motivation.
In particular, it is important that both BCGP and GPPV theories are “decorated” TQFTs, with additional structure
\omega
or
\mathfrak{s}\in\operatorname{Spin}^{c}(M)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
originating from the equivariance under
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\subset G_{\mathbb{C}}
, the maximal torus of
G_{\mathbb{C}}={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
.

Taking into account these extra structures, the first order approximation to
{\mathcal{H}}\big(T^{2}\big)
in our theories is as follows. From cutting and gluing (surgery) rules, we infer that the space
{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{GPPV}}\big(T^{2}\big)
has basis
|n,x\rangle_{a}
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
x\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
and
a
is a relative spin
c
structure. It is often convenient to replace
x
by a dual variable
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, so that the Weyl group of
G_{\mathbb{C}}={\rm SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
maps
|n,m\rangle_{a}\mapsto|{-}n,-m\rangle_{a}
. Therefore, for each given spin
c
structure (= choice of background), we have

{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{GPPV}}^{(a)}\big(T^{2}\big)\cong\mathbb{C}\bigg[\frac{\Lambda\times\Lambda^{\vee}}{W}\bigg],

(5.1)

where, as usual, by
\mathbb{C}[S]
we denote the space of complex valued functions
16
16

16

In principle, one has to impose a certain asymptotic behavior condition on the functions on the lattice. This condition should be coherent with a certain continuity condition on the allowed functions on the dual space, related by Fourier transform. In this work we do not specify such conditions.
on set
S
. Note that, compared to the Hilbert space
{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{WRT}}\big(T^{2}\big)=\mathbb{C}[{\Lambda}/({W\times k\Lambda})]
, the space of states (
5.1
) has two copies of the lattice, i.e., corresponds to a
toroidal
algebra, and has no cut-off due to the level
[
20
,
40
]
. The latter property is, of course, anticipated
a priori
since the invariants
\widehat{Z}_{b}(M;q)
depend on
q
, generic with
|q|<1
. From the Kazhdan–Lusztig correspondence and the theory of
W
-algebras, it is also natural to attribute this “doubling” to a larger symmetry associated with the action of two (quantum) groups, such that (
5.1
) is basically a product of root lattices for
G
and its Langlands dual
{}^{L}G
.

At a similar level of approximation, for a fixed choice of the “decoration”/equivariant parameter
\alpha
, we have

{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{BCGP}}^{(\alpha)}\big(T^{2}\big)\cong\mathbb{C}[H_{r}],\qquad H_{r}:=\{1-r,3-r,\ldots,r-1\},

which is a direct consequence of the corresponding statement for categories
\mathcal{C}=\oplus_{\overline{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}_{\overline{\alpha}}

[
12
]
.
It is very well known that many affine algebras and VOAs at “critical level” have large center. The same is true about quantum groups at roots of unity. Very often, the center has a nice geometric meaning as the space of functions on some variety (moduli space)
\mathcal{M}
, and in the case at hand
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Below we refine these descriptions of
{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{BCGP}}\big(T^{2}\big)
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{GPPV}}\big(T^{2}\big)
by looking at these spaces from various angles.

Before continuing with
\Sigma=T^{2}
, it is instructive to pause for a moment and consider simpler cases of
\Sigma=S^{2}
or
S^{2}
with two points removed. In a 3d TQFT associated to
\mathcal{C}
, objects in
\mathcal{C}
correspond to line operators whereas their non-trivial extensions are represented by non-trivial junctions, i.e., local operators where two line operators meet, illustrated in Figure
3
. The space of such local operators is the space of states on
\Sigma=S^{2}\setminus\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
; in particular, it is non-trivial for BCGP theory indicating the non-semisimple nature of the TQFT. As a special case, the space of local operators at the junction of two trivial lines is simply
{\mathcal{H}}\big(S^{2}\big)
, and for GPPV theory its structure is conveniently encoded in the unreduced
17
17

17

This is the version that includes the contribution of the Cartan component of the adjoint chiral, e.g.,
\widehat{Z}_{0}^{(\text{unred})}\big(S^{3}\big)=\frac{1}{(t^{2}q^{2};q)_{\infty}}
for
G=\operatorname{SU}(2)
. The reduced version is obtained by removing this contribution, i.e., via multiplying by
(qt;q)_{\infty}
. Moreover, there is also a factor of
(1;q)_{\infty}
in the numerator which requires regularization. Sometimes it is simply removed. And, sometimes only the zero mode is removed so that
(1;q)_{\infty}
is replaced by
(q;q)_{\infty}
, as in (
5.2
).
version of the
\widehat{Z}
-invariant for
S^{1}\times S^{2}
:

\frac{1}{2}\frac{(x;q)_{\infty}(q;q)_{\infty}\big(x^{-1};q\big)_{\infty}}{(qtx;q)_{\infty}(qt;q)_{\infty}\big(qtx^{-1};q\big)_{\infty}},

(5.2)

where the refinement variable
t
corresponds to homological-type grading in the context of categorification.
In general, in a Rozansky–Witten theory the space
{\mathcal{H}}\big(S^{2}\big)=\mathbb{C}[X]
encodes the geometry of the target space
X
, and the partition function on
S^{1}\times S^{2}
can be identified with the Hilbert series of
X
. In the case of
\widehat{Z}
theory, there is a manifest symmetry between the numerator and the denominator of the above expression. It is typical for a partition function of the cotangent bundles,
X=T^{*}M
, where the numerator and denominator correspond to the fiber and base, respectively. Indeed, “half” of the expression (
5.2
) is precisely the Hilbert series of the affine Grassmannian, whereas the complete expression (
5.2
) is the Hilbert series of a model for
T^{*}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
that was used in
[
39
]
.

Figure 3:
A sphere with two punctures surrounding a local operator at the junction of two line operators.

Recall, that the affine Grassmannian for
G=\operatorname{SU}(2)
has two connected components, with a similar Morse cell complex. In each component, the transverse slices are labeled by a pair of non-negative integers,
m
and
n
. In particular, the transverse slices with
n=0
form a family of hyper-Kähler manifolds with quaternionic dimension
m^{2}
that provide a finite-dimensional approximation to
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
. Their Hilbert series is

\frac{\big(q^{m+1};q\big)_{m}}{(qx;q)_{m}(q;q)_{m}\big(qx^{-1};q\big)_{m}}.

In the limit
m\to\infty
, we recover
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
itself (or, more precisely, one of its connected components) and the denominator of the formula (
5.2
) at
t=1
.

Now, returning to
\Sigma=T^{2}
, let
D^{b}\operatorname{Coh}(X)
be the (bounded) derived category of coherent sheaves on
X
. Then, the proposal of
[
39
]
that the GPPV TQFT can be equivalently formulated as the Rozansky–Witten theory with the target space
T^{*}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
implies, among other things,

{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{GPPV}}\big(T^{2}\big)\cong K^{0}\big(D^{b}_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{T}}\operatorname{Coh}(T^{*}\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\big),

(5.3)

where
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
is the affine Grassmannian of
G
or, rather, the affine Grassmannian of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
to be more precise. If the left-hand side, here is understood as the Hilbert (
5.1
) used in the cutting-and-gluing (surgery) formulae
[
40
]
, then (
5.3
) is a priori not obvious at all. We shortly demonstrate that it is indeed the case, but also point out that producing similar arguments for other ingredients of GPPV theory is necessary to establish the equivalence of different definitions, such as the explicit integral formula for plumbed manifolds and the mathematically rigorous (but extremely hard to compute) definition based on the Rozansky–Witten theory with the affine Grassmannian as the target space.

Returning to the proof of (
5.3
), first one needs to clarify what exactly is meant by
T^{*}\mathrm{Gr}_{G}
which, for general
G
, is a singular infinite-dimensional space. Luckily, the “space of triples”
[
14
,
15
,
63
]
provides the right candidate for the total space of this cotangent bundle. Let
\text{St}_{G}
be the affine Grassmannian analogue of the Steinberg variety. Then
[
9
]

K^{G(O)}(\text{St}_{G})\cong\mathbb{C}\big[\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\times{}^{L}\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\big]^{W}

and

\operatorname{Spec}K\big(D^{b}\operatorname{Coh}^{G(O)}_{\mathrm{St}_{G}}(T^{*}\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\big)\cong\operatorname{Spec}K^{G(O)}(\mathrm{Gr}_{G})\cong\frac{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\times{}^{L}\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}}{W},

where
O=\mathbb{C}[[t]]
,
F=\mathbb{C}((t))
, and
\mathrm{Gr}_{G}=G(F)/G(O)
. Enhancing the equivariant
G(O)
-action to
G(O)\times\operatorname{U}(1)
action, where
\operatorname{U}(1)
acts by loop rotation, corresponds to a non-commutative deformation (quantization) of this space. Up to a two-fold cover, this is precisely the quantization of the space
\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}\big(G_{\mathbb{C}},T^{2}\big)\cong\frac{\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}}{W}
, thus demonstrating that the left-hand side of (
5.3
) given by (
5.1
) indeed is equal to the right-hand side of (
5.3
) computed geometrically.

5.1.1
Quantization

The Hilbert space of Chern–Simons theory and its close cousins on a surface
\Sigma
can be obtained by quantizing a suitable “phase space”
M
, that in many interesting examples can be realized as a submanifold in the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{H}(G,\Sigma)\cong\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(G_{\mathbb{C}},\Sigma)
. For example, a “real slice”
M=\mathcal{M}_{\text{flat}}(G_{\mathbb{R}},\Sigma)
that corresponds to a real form
G_{\mathbb{R}}
of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
gives the Hilbert space of “
G_{\mathbb{R}}
Chern–Simons theory” (closely related to the Teichmüller theory
[
2
]
) whereas
M=\mathcal{M}_{H}(G,\Sigma)
gives the Hilbert space of
\widehat{Z}
theory (which provides a non-perturbative definition to what one might call a “
G_{\mathbb{C}}
Chern–Simons theory”). In all of these cases, we can represent
{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma)
as a Hom-space (
=
space of open strings) in the category of branes on
\mathcal{M}_{H}(G,\Sigma)

[
46
]
:

{\mathcal{H}}(\Sigma)=\operatorname{Hom}({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{B}}_{cc}),

(5.4)

where
{\mathcal{B}}_{cc}
is a rank-1 brane that carries a line bundle of curvature
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=\omega
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
is supported on
M
. Note, at the current stage of development, the definition of the Fukaya category does not include coisotropic objects. However, as pointed out in
[
46
]
, the space (
5.4
) is best computed (and also defined!) in the derived category of coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{M}_{H}(G,\Sigma)
. Indeed, both
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{cc}
are objects in
D^{b}\operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{M}_{H}(G,\Sigma))
, which is mathematically well defined and is much easier to work with than the Fukaya category. Then, in the case of compact
G
, the space (
5.4
) recovers
18
18

18

See
[
46
]
for details.
the standard result
[
47
,
77
]
of the geometric quantization
[
8
,
54
,
70
]
, and in the complex case of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
recovers the Hilbert space of the GPPV TQFT (
5.1
). For compact
G
, there is a large body of work, that includes
[
4
,
57
,
71
]
, showing that the action of the mapping class group is equivalent to the one in the Reshetikhin–Turaev construction
[
69
]
, whereas in the complex case of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
the mapping class group action has been studied in detail only in genus 1
[
20
,
40
]
.

In the case of BCGP invariants, we have
[
12
, Remark 5.10]

\dim{\mathcal{H}}_{\text{BCGP}}(\Sigma)=\begin{cases}r^{\prime}&\text{if}\quad g=1,\\
r^{3g-3}&\text{if}\quad g>1~\text{and}~r~\text{is odd},\\
\frac{r^{3g-3}}{2^{g-1}}&\text{if}\quad g>1~\text{and}~r~\text{is even}.\end{cases}

Recall that
r^{\prime}
is
r
(resp.
\frac{r}{2}
) when
r
is odd (resp. even).
The choice of a background (“decoration”) that appears in
\mathrm{N}_{r}
and
\widehat{Z}
invariants is of the same type as in
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}
-crossed modules, thus allowing to describe both TQFTs also in the language of
\mathbb{T}_{\mathbb{C}}
-crossed modules.

5.1.2
A prototypical example

Consider bosonic Chern–Simons theory with gauge group
\operatorname{U}(1)
and level
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. It has a topological
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
(0-form) global symmetry. On the level of the path integral, on a closed 3-manifold
M
, it can be coupled to a flat
19
19

19

Restriction of connections being flat can be interpreted as considering discrete topology on
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
symmetry group.
background
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
connection

\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Hom}\big(H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}\big)

as follows:

Z_{\operatorname{U}(1)_{r}}(M,\omega)=\int DA\exp\bigg\{\frac{{\rm i}r}{2\pi}\int_{M}AdA+2\pi{\rm i}\omega(c_{1})\bigg\},

(5.5)

where
c_{1}\in H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z})
is the first Chern class of the
\operatorname{U}(1)
gauge connection (locally represented by 1-form
A
). On the level of charge/charged operators this 0-form global symmetry can be understood locally as follows (i.e., as in the general setting of
[
37
]
).
Charged
operators are 0-dimensional. An operator with charge
m\in\mathbb{Z}
can be understood as the puncture in the spacetime with magnetic flux
m=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{S^{2}}F
over a small 2-sphere
S^{2}
surrounding the puncture (i.e., monopole). A
charge
operator
O_{g}(\Sigma)
corresponding to a group element
g\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
and supported on a 2-dimensional surface
\Sigma
is simply

O_{g}(\Sigma)=\exp\bigg({\rm i}{g}\int_{\Sigma}F\bigg).

Globally, turning on a non-trivial
\omega
in (
5.5
) can be realized by the insertion of the charge operator supported on a 2-chain representing the Poincaré dual of
\omega
, with charges on simplices given by the corresponding coefficients in
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
. The
\operatorname{U}(1)_{r}
Chern–Simons TQFT, “enriched” by this
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
global symmetry, then can be described in terms of a
G
-crossed MTC
\mathcal{C}
, for
G=\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
, using the general formalism of
[
5
]
(cf. also
[
7
]
) as follows. In the decomposition

\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{g\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}_{g},

the component
\mathcal{C}_{g}
has simple objects that correspond to the line operators

W_{e}(\gamma)=\exp\bigg({\rm i}e\int_{\gamma}A\bigg)

with a complex charge
e
with the fixed value
e\bmod 1=g\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
. For any fixed
g
there are exactly
r
distinct simple objects, as
W_{r}(\gamma)
is known to be a trivial operator on the quantum level. That is, an equivalence class of the operator is determined by the value
e\bmod r\in\mathbb{C}/r\mathbb{Z}
. The fusion of the line operators is obviously consistent with the grading on the category.

Note that for
g\neq 0
, such line operators are not the ordinary line operators. They are not well defined by themselves, but are only allowed to live on a boundary of a surface
\Sigma
(i.e., locally
\gamma=\partial\Sigma
) where a charge operator
O_{g}(\Sigma)
is supported.

5.2
Decorated TQFTs, gradings, and Fourier transform

Here we consider some basic operations on decorated TQFTs. We propose that BCGP and GPPV TQFTs are related by the combination of such operations (with slight modification related to the simplifications we will impose below). Namely, for
r=2\bmod 4
, BCGP TQFT can be obtained by applying “Fourier transform” followed by “
r
-wrapping” to GPPV TQFT. For
r=\pm 1\bmod 4
, the full transform is more involved, in particular on the level of the partition functions there is an extra summation over
\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(M,\mathbb{Z})
in Conjectures
1.2
and
1.5
. Note that in this relation, at all intermediate steps
r
and
q
should be considered as independent parameters. Only at the final step, one has to take the radial limit
q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}
(which, may lead to some divergences in certain cases). For the purpose of a more transparent exposition, instead of spin
c
-TQFTs we consider
H^{2}(\cdot;\mathbb{Z})
-decorated spin-TQFTs, and instead of
H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
-decorated TQFTs we consider
H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
-decorated TQFTs. In the rest of the section, we study general TQFTs decorated by the structures as above and operations between them. We hope this discussion can be useful for other applications of decorated TQFTs, beyond the scope of the present paper.

5.2.1
Decorated TQFTs and grading of Hilbert spaces

“Decorated” TQFTs (i.e., TQFTs defined on bordisms with additional structure) in general have induced grading on the vector spaces
V(\Sigma)
associated to codimension-1 closed manifolds
\Sigma
(i.e., the objects of the bordism category). The general rule is that the choice of the decoration on
\Sigma\times S^{1}
decomposes into a choice of decoration on
\Sigma
and a choice of the parameter
dual
to the grading on
V(\Sigma)
. In particular, if the decoration on
\Sigma\times S^{1}
is just a pullback of the decoration on
\Sigma
(with respect to the projection
\Sigma\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
), then
Z\big(\Sigma\times S^{1}\big)=\dim V(\Sigma)
, where the right-hand side is the total dimension (over all gradings).

3d
\bm{H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}
-decorated TQFTs

These are functors on the version of the category of
H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
-decorated cobordisms considered in
[
12
, Sections 3.1 and 3.2]
with some additional data forgotten and the group
\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
replaced by
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. The objects are closed surfaces
\Sigma
with a distinguished point on each connected component and decorated by a class in
H^{1}(\Sigma,*;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, where
*
denotes the set of distinguished points. The morphisms are 3-dimensional cobordisms decorated by
H^{1}(M,*;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
where
*
are the set of the distinguished points on the boundary component.

The choice of the decoration on
\Sigma\times S^{1}
is an element of

\displaystyle H^{1}\big(\Sigma\times S^{1};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\cong H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}(H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}).

So
V(\Sigma)
is naturally graded by
H_{0}(\Sigma)\cong H^{2}(\Sigma)
. The graded dimensions are given by the following relation:

\sum_{n\in H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})}\dim V_{n}(\Sigma,\omega){\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\alpha(n)}=Z\big(\Sigma\times S^{1},\omega\oplus\alpha\big),

(5.6)

where
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma)
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}(H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
.

Physically,
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
is a 0-form symmetry. There are 0-dimensional charged operators with charges in
\mathbb{Z}
and 2-dimensional charge operators labelled by
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, as in the example in Section
5.1.2
. Turning on
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}(H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
above corresponds to insertion of a charge operator along the spatial slice
\Sigma
.

3d
\bm{H^{2}(\cdot;\mathbb{Z})}
-decorated TQFTs

The choice of the decoration on
\Sigma\times S^{1}
is an element of

H^{2}\big(\Sigma\times S^{1};\mathbb{Z}\big)\cong H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}).

So
V(\Sigma)
is naturally graded by
\operatorname{Hom}(H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
. The graded dimensions are given by the following relation:

\sum_{\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}\dim V_{\omega}(\Sigma,n){\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\omega(\gamma)}=Z\big(\Sigma\times S^{1},n\oplus\gamma\big),

(5.7)

where
n\in H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
and
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. Since
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is in general not discrete, one has to specify what is meant by
\sum_{\omega}
. Consider the case of connected
\Sigma
(the generalization to the disconnected case is straightforward). Let
g
be the genus of
\Sigma
. Then
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\cong(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{2g}=\big(S^{1}\big)^{2g}
, but non-canonically. There is a unique homogeneous form
\mu(\omega)\in\Omega^{2g}\big(H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\big)
normalized such that
\int\mu(\omega)=1
. Then

\sum_{\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}\ldots:=\int_{H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}\mu(\omega)\ldots

It also satisfies

\int_{H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}\mu(\omega){\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\omega(\gamma)}=\delta_{\gamma}:=\begin{cases}1,&\gamma=0,\\
0,&\gamma\neq 0,\end{cases}

where
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
.

Physically,
\mathbb{Z}
is a 1-form symmetry. There are 1-dimensional charged operators with charges in
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and 1-dimensional charge operators labelled by
\mathbb{Z}
. Turning on
\gamma\in H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
above corresponds to insertion of a charge operator supported on a 1-dimensional curve in the spatial slice
\Sigma
.

5.2.2
Fourier transform of TQFTs

In this section, we suppose we are given two TQFTs, called
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
defined on the categories of cobordisms decorated respectively by
H^{2}(\cdot;\mathbb{Z})
and by
H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
classes, and we suppose that their partition functions on a closed 3-manifold
Y
are related by a Fourier transform:

Z^{\prime}(Y,\omega)=\sum_{b\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\int_{Y}\omega\cup b}Z(Y,b),

(5.8)

where
\omega\in H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
.
Such Fourier transform in particular appears as one of the two steps in the linear transform relating CGP and GPPV invariants in Conjecture
1.7
, with the second step being the “
r
-wrapping” that will be considered later.

We then try to extend this Fourier transform to the full category of cobordisms.
We first observe that, assuming all the integrals converge, a natural candidate for the inverse transform is

Z(Y,b)=\frac{1}{|\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})|}\int_{H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}\mu(\omega){\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\int_{Y}\omega\cup b}Z^{\prime}(Y,\omega),

(5.9)

where
\mu(\omega)
is the homogeneous top degree form on
H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\times(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{b_{1}}
uniquely fixed by the condition that
\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mu(\omega)=1

for each connected component
\mathcal{M}
inside
H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
. The relation between the values of TQFT on a closed 2-manifold
\Sigma
is given by the swap of grading with decoration

V^{\prime}_{n}(\Sigma,\omega)=V_{\omega}(\Sigma,n),

(5.10)

where
n\in H^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
,
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
and the vector spaces are assumed to be finite dimensional. It is easy to see that (
5.10
) is consistent with (
5.6
), (
5.7
) combined with (
5.8
), (
5.9
).

In order to extend the relation between the TQFTs to cobordisms, let us define the category of
H^{2}
-decorated cobordisms as follows:

•

The objects are pairs
(\Sigma,b)
with
b\in H^{2}(\Sigma,\Sigma\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z})
, where
\{*\}
is the datum of one base point per connected component of
\Sigma
.

•

A morphism from
(\Sigma_{-},b_{-})
to
(\Sigma_{+},b_{+})
is a pair
(M,b)
with
b\in H^{2}(M,\partial M\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z})
, where
\partial M=\Sigma_{+}\sqcup\overline{\Sigma_{-}}
,
\{*\}=\{*_{-}\}\sqcup\{*_{+}\}
is the set of one basepoint per component of
\Sigma_{-}\sqcup\Sigma_{+}
and
b_{\pm}
is the restriction of
b
to
\Sigma_{\pm}
.

The composition of
M_{1}\colon(\Sigma_{-},b_{-})\to(\Sigma_{0},b_{0})
and
M_{2}\colon(\Sigma_{0},b_{0})\to(\Sigma_{+},b_{+})
is obtained by gluing
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
along
\Sigma_{0}
and defining
b
on
M
as
r(\phi^{-1}(b_{-}+b_{+}))
, where
\{*\}=\{*_{+},*_{-},*_{0}\}
,

\phi\colon\ H^{2}(M,\partial M\cup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*\})\to H^{2}(M_{-},\partial M_{-}\setminus\{*_{-},*_{0}\})\oplus H^{2}(M_{+},\partial M_{+}\setminus\{*_{+},*_{0}\})

is coming from the Mayer–Vietoris sequence and

r\colon\ H^{2}(M,\partial M\cup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{+},*_{0},*_{-}\})\to H^{2}(M,\partial M\setminus(\{*_{+},*_{-}\}))

is the restriction map induced by the long exact sequence of the triple

(M,(\partial M\cup\Sigma_{0})\setminus\{*_{0},*_{+},*_{-}\},\partial M\setminus\{*_{+},*_{-}\}).

We shall now show that
V(\Sigma,b)
is endowed by a
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
-grading as follows.
First of all remark that

H^{2}(\Sigma\times[0,1],(\Sigma\setminus\{*\})\times\{0,1\};\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{1}(\Sigma,\{*\};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(\Sigma,\Sigma\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z}),

where the injection
\delta\colon H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{1}(\Sigma,\{*\};\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(\Sigma\times[0,1],(\Sigma\setminus\{*\})\times\{0,1\};\mathbb{Z})
is induced by the exact sequence of the triple
(\Sigma\times[0,1],(\Sigma\setminus\{*\})\times\{0,1\},(\Sigma\setminus\{*\})\times\{0\}))
; and the generators
H^{2}(\Sigma,\Sigma\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{\pi_{0}(\Sigma)}
are the Poincaré duals of the arcs
\{p\}\times[0,1]
for
p\in\{*\}
.

Given
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, the
\omega
-homogeneous subspace of
Z(\Sigma,b)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle V(\Sigma,b)_{\omega}:

\displaystyle=\big\{x\in V(\Sigma,b)\mid\forall c\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\ Z(\Sigma\times[0,1],b+\delta(c))(x)=\exp(-2\pi{\rm i}\omega(c))x\big\}

(remark that the restriction of
b+\delta(c)
to
\Sigma\times\{0\}
is
b
).

Now observe that there is a well defined map

\int_{M}\colon\ H^{1}(M,\{*\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\otimes H^{2}(M,\partial M\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

defined equivalently as
\int_{M}\omega\otimes b:=\langle\omega\cup b,[M]\rangle=\omega(PD(b))
, where
[M]\in H^{3}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z})
is the fundamental class and
PD
is Poincaré duality.

Considering all the morphisms
M\colon(\Sigma_{-},b_{-})\to(\Sigma_{+},b_{+})
for which the underlying manifold is
M
we define their Fourier transform for any
\omega\in H^{1}(M,\{*\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
as

Z^{\prime}(M,\omega)_{b_{-}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in H^{2}(M,\partial M\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z})/\delta(H^{1}(\Sigma_{-};\mathbb{Z}))\\
b|_{\Sigma_{-}}=b_{-}\end{subarray}}\exp\bigg(2\pi{\rm i}\int_{M}\omega\cup b\bigg)Z_{\omega}(M,b),

where

(i)

Z_{\omega}(M,b)\colon V_{\omega_{-}}(\Sigma_{-},b_{-})\to V_{\omega_{+}}(\Sigma_{+},b_{+})

is the restriction of
Z(M,b)
to the degree
\omega_{\pm}=\omega|_{\Sigma_{\pm}}
vector subspaces and we use the identification
V_{\omega_{\pm}}(\Sigma_{\pm},b_{\pm})=V^{\prime}_{b_{\pm}}(\Sigma_{\pm},\omega_{\pm})
to interpret it as a map

V^{\prime}_{b_{-}}(\Sigma_{-},\omega_{-})\to V^{\prime}_{b_{+}}(\Sigma_{+},\omega_{+})
;

(ii)

\delta\colon H^{1}(\Sigma_{-};\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(M,\partial M\setminus\{*\};\mathbb{Z})

is induced as above by the exact sequence of the triple
(M,\partial M\setminus\{*\},\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\})
;

(iii)

the sum is over all the representatives of classes
b
restricting to
b_{-}
on
\Sigma_{-}
and the choice of a representative is irrelevant because we have:

\displaystyle\exp\bigg(2\pi{\rm i}\int_{M}\omega\cup(b+\delta(c))\bigg)Z_{\omega}(M,b+\delta(c))

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\bigg(2\pi{\rm i}\int_{M}\omega\cup b+\omega\cup\delta(c)\bigg)Z_{\omega}(M,b)\circ Z_{\omega}(\Sigma_{-}\times[0,1],b_{-}+\delta(c))

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\bigg(2\pi{\rm i}\int_{M}\omega\cup b\bigg)\exp(2\pi{\rm i}\omega(c))Z_{\omega}(M,b)\exp(-2\pi{\rm i}\omega(c))

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\bigg(2\pi{\rm i}\int_{M}\omega\cup b\bigg)Z_{\omega}(M,b),

where in the second equality we used the definition of the grading on
V(\Sigma,b)
.

Of course, in the above formula we assume that the sum is convergent.
So given a cobordism
M\colon\Sigma_{-}\to\Sigma_{+}
and
\omega\in H^{1}(M,\{*\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
we will from now on say that
Z^{\prime}(M,\omega)
exists if it exists for all
b_{-}\in H^{2}(\Sigma_{-},\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\};\mathbb{Z})
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose that
M_{-}\colon\Sigma_{-}\to\Sigma_{0}
and
M_{+}\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{+}
are two cobordisms and let
M=M_{+}\circ M_{-}
. Let
\omega_{\pm}\in H^{1}(M_{\pm},\{*_{0},*_{\pm}\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})

and
\omega\in H^{1}(M,\{*_{+},*_{-}\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
be defined as
\operatorname{res}(\omega^{\prime})
, where
\omega^{\prime}\in H^{1}(M,\{*_{-},*_{+},*_{0}\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
restricts to both
\omega_{\pm}
, and

\operatorname{res}\colon H^{1}(M,\{*_{-},*_{+},*_{0}\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\to H^{1}(M,\{*_{-},*_{+}\};\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})

is the restriction map.
If
Z^{\prime}(M_{\pm},\omega_{\pm})
exist, then also
Z^{\prime}(M,\omega)
exists and it holds

Z^{\prime}(M,\omega)=Z^{\prime}(M_{+},\omega_{+})\circ Z^{\prime}(M_{-},\omega_{-}).

Proof.

Let
b\in H^{2}(M,\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\}\cup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{+}\};\mathbb{Z})
.
By the exact sequence of the triple

(M,\Sigma_{-}\sqcup\Sigma_{0}\sqcup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{-},*_{0},*_{+}\},\Sigma_{-}\sqcup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{-},*_{+}\}),

we have a surjective map

\pi\colon\ H^{2}(M,\Sigma_{-}\sqcup\Sigma_{0}\sqcup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{-},*_{0},*_{+}\};\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(M,\Sigma_{-}\sqcup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{-},*_{+}\};\mathbb{Z})

so that
\pi^{-1}(b)
is well defined up to elements of the form
\delta(c)
for some
c\in H^{1}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\});\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
where
\delta
is induced by the same exact sequence.
So if
b^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(b)
then there are well-defined restrictions
{\rm res}_{\pm}(b^{\prime})\in H^{2}(M_{\pm},\Sigma_{\pm}\setminus\{*_{\pm}\}\sqcup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\};\mathbb{Z})
. Furthermore, one can check that the restrictions to
H^{2}(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\};\mathbb{Z})
of
{\rm res}_{+}(b^{\prime})
and of
{\rm res}_{-}(b^{\prime})
coincide and depend only on
b
(not on the choice of
b^{\prime}
). Let us then denote this common restriction
b_{0}\in H^{2}(\Sigma_{0},\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\};\mathbb{Z})
.
We have then

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}(M,\omega)_{b_{-}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\frac{H^{2}(M,\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\}\cup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{+}\};\mathbb{Z})}{\delta(H^{1}(\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\};\mathbb{Z}))}\\
{\rm res}_{-}(b)=b_{-}\end{subarray}}\exp\bigg(2{\rm i}\pi\int_{M}\omega\cup b\bigg)Z(M,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b^{\prime}\in\frac{H^{2}(M,\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\}\cup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\}\cup\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{+}\};\mathbb{Z})}{\delta(H^{1}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\}\sqcup\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\};\mathbb{Z}))}\\
{\rm res}_{-}(\pi(b^{\prime}))=b_{-}\end{subarray}}\exp\bigg(2{\rm i}\pi\int_{M}\omega\cup\pi(b^{\prime})\bigg)Z(M,\pi(b^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\sum\exp\bigg(2{\rm i}\pi\int_{M_{-}}\omega_{-}\cup b^{\prime}_{-}+\int_{M_{+}}\omega_{+}\cup b^{\prime}_{+}\bigg)Z(M,b^{\prime}_{+})Z(M,b^{\prime}_{-}),

where the last sum ranges over

b^{\prime}_{-}\in H^{2}(M_{-},\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\}\cup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\};\mathbb{Z})/\delta\big(H^{1}(\Sigma_{-}\setminus\{*_{-}\};\mathbb{Z})\big)

such that
{\rm res}_{-}(\pi(b^{\prime}_{-}))=b_{-}
and over

b^{\prime}_{+}\in H^{2}(M_{+},\Sigma_{+}\setminus\{*_{+}\}\cup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\};\mathbb{Z})/\delta\big(H^{1}(\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{0}\};\mathbb{Z})\big)

such that
{\rm res}_{0}(\pi(b^{\prime}_{+}))={\rm res}_{0}(\pi(b^{\prime}_{-}))
(and where we let
b^{\prime}_{\pm}
be the restriction of
b^{\prime}
to
(M_{\pm},\Sigma_{\pm}\sqcup\Sigma_{0}\setminus\{*_{\pm},*_{0}\})
.
The last equality uses the Mayer–Vietoris sequence for the pairs
(M,\Sigma_{+,-,0}\setminus\{*_{+,-,0}\})
and
(M,\Sigma_{\pm,0}\setminus\{*_{\pm,0}\})
and the fact that
Z
is functorial.
∎

5.2.3
“
\bm{r}
-wrapping” of
\bm{H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}

The other operation which we will need to upgrade the relation (
3.14
) to TQFTs is the operation that takes an
H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
-TQFT
Z
and produces another
H^{1}(\cdot;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
-TQFT
Z^{\prime}
. For closed connected manifolds, we have (assume
r=2\bmod 4
):

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}(M,\omega)={}

\displaystyle\frac{r^{-(b_{0}+b_{1})/2}}{\sqrt{|\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})}\mu(\alpha){\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}q_{s}(\alpha^{\prime})-2\pi{\rm i}{\ell k}(\alpha^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}r^{b_{1}}\delta(r\alpha^{\prime\prime}-\omega^{\prime\prime}){Z}(M,\alpha),

(5.11)

where we choose an explicit splitting

\omega=\omega^{\prime}\oplus\omega^{\prime\prime},\qquad\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}\oplus\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Tor}H_{1}(M;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\oplus(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{b_{1}}

(5.12)

as in Section
3.5
.

Applying the formula (
5.11
) to
M=\Sigma\times S^{1}
, where
\Sigma
is a closed connected oriented genus
g
surface, we get the following relation between the dimensions of the corresponding graded vector spaces:

\dim V^{\prime}_{n}(\Sigma,\omega)=r^{-g}\sum_{m\in(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z})^{2g}}\dim V_{rn}\bigg(\Sigma,\frac{\omega+m}{r}\bigg),

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\cong(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{2g}
.

This formula suggests the following generalisation to TQFTs. Assume that after a choice of a symplectic basis in
H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
(that splits the generators into
g
A-cycles and
g
B-cycles) and the corresponding splitting
\omega=\omega_{A}\oplus\omega_{B}
(
\omega_{A,B}\in(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{g}
), one can identify
V_{n}(\Sigma,\omega_{A}\oplus\omega_{B})
for a fixed
\omega_{A}
and all possible
\omega_{B}
. That is one can explicitly drop the dependence on
\omega_{B}
:
V_{n}(\Sigma,\omega_{A}):=V_{n}(\Sigma,\omega_{A}\oplus\omega_{B})
. Then the same is true for
V^{\prime}_{n}(\Sigma,\omega_{A}\oplus\omega_{B})
, and we have

V^{\prime}_{n}(\Sigma,\omega_{A})=\bigoplus_{s\in(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z})^{g}}V_{rn}\bigg(\Sigma,\frac{\omega_{A}+s}{r}\bigg),

where
(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z})^{g}\subset(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{g}
is identified with the subgroup of holonomies of order
r
along the A-cycles.

This is consistent with the conjectural relation between the CGP and
\widehat{Z}
invariants of knots (which are valued in the corresponding vector spaces above for
\Sigma=T^{2}
).

The extension of the relation (
5.11
) to general bordisms turns out to be subtle and technically complicated. One of the reasons is that one requires to choose a splitting of
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
in (
5.12
), which is not a natural structure on manifolds and there are various ways one can extend it to the manifolds with boundary. We will not address this issue in this work.

6
Relation to WRT invariants

In the previous sections, we focused on the relations between
\widehat{Z}
and
\mathrm{N}_{r}
. But there are also relations between and
\widehat{Z}
and
\operatorname{WRT}
invariants and between
\mathrm{N}_{r}
and
\operatorname{WRT}
invariants (possibly in their cohomology-refined versions). In this section, we compare this triangle of relations and show that they are compatible with each other.

6.1
Compatibility of the relations between
\bm{{\rm N}^{0}_{r}}
,
\bm{{\rm WRT}_{r}}
, and
\bm{\widehat{Z}}

Let
M
be a rational homology sphere. Consider

Z_{a}^{{\rm SO}(3)}(M):=\sum_{\mathfrak{s}\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(M)}S_{a,\mathfrak{s}}^{{{\rm SO}(3)}}\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{s}}(M),\qquad a\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}),

where

\displaystyle S^{{{\rm SO}(3)}}_{a,\sigma(b,s)}:=\frac{1}{{|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}}\begin{cases}\sum_{f}{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\left(-\frac{r-1}{4}{\ell k}(a,a)+{\ell k}(a,f-b)+{\ell k}(f,f)-\frac{1}{4}\mu(M,s)\right)}&\text{if}~r=1~\text{mod}~4,\\[5.69054pt]
\sqrt{|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}q_{s}(a)-2\pi{\rm i}{\ell k}(a,b)}&\text{if}~r=2~\text{mod}~4,\\[5.69054pt]
\sum_{f}{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\left(-\frac{r+1}{4}{\ell k}(a,a)-{\ell k}(a,f+b)-{\ell k}(f,f)-\frac{1}{4}\mu(M,s)\right)}&\text{if}~r=3~\text{mod}~4.\end{cases}

We have introduced
\operatorname{SO}(3)
subscript in order to explicitly distinguish
Z_{a}^{{\rm SO}(3)}(M)
from
Z_{a}(M)
that appeared in
[
41
,
43
,
44
]
. The latter naturally appear in the decomposition of the standard WRT invariant (corresponding to
\operatorname{SU}(2)
Chern–Simons gauge theory), while the former, as we will see below, appear naturally in the decomposition of its mod-2-cohomology refined version, which can be understood as
\operatorname{SO}(3)
version of Chern–Simons gauge theory. In physical terms, the refinement parameter
\omega\in H^{1}(M,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
appears in the action term
\pi\int_{M}\omega\cup w_{2}
where
w_{2}\in H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
is the second Stiefel–Whitney class of the
\operatorname{SO}(3)
gauge bundle.

Conjecture
1.2
can be written as follows (for any
r\neq 0\bmod 4
):

\mathrm{N}_{r}(M,\omega)=(-1)^{r}\mathcal{T}(M,[\omega])\sum_{a\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\omega(a)}Z^{{\rm SO}(3)}_{a}(M)\Big|_{q={\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}

(6.1)

for
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})\setminus H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.
Similarly, for the
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
-refined WRT invariant of
[
25
]
it is conjectured (see Appendix
B
for details):

\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)=\frac{1}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}}\sum_{a\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\omega(a)}Z^{{\rm SO}(3)}_{a}(M)\Big|_{q={\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}

(6.2)

for
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.

Given
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
, as shown in
[
24
]
, a normalized invariant
N_{r}^{0}(M,\omega)
can be defined by

\mathrm{N}_{r}^{0}(M,\omega):=\frac{\mathrm{N}_{r}(M\#M^{\prime},\omega\oplus\omega^{\prime})}{\mathrm{N}_{r}(M^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}

(6.3)

for any
M^{\prime}
and
\omega^{\prime}\in H^{1}(M^{\prime};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
such that both denominator and numerator in the right-hand side are well defined and moreover the denominator is non-zero. For
\omega\notin H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, we have

\mathrm{N}_{r}^{0}(M,\omega)=0.

And for
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, the conjecture of
[
25
]
in this paper’s normalisation (theorem for knot surgeries) is that
20
20

20

For
b_{1}>0
, it is assumed that
|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|=0
.

\mathrm{N}_{r}^{0}(M,\omega)=D\cdot|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega).

(6.4)

The statement of the above conjecture follows from
[
28
, Theorem 1.4]
and
[
18
, Theorem 1]
at least for
r
odd and for
\omega=0
.

Let us check that (
6.4
) is consistent with our conjectures (
6.1
) and (
6.2
). Plugging (
6.1
) into the definition (
6.3
) we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}_{r}^{0}(M,\omega)=\frac{\mathcal{T}(M\#M^{\prime},[\omega]\oplus[\omega^{\prime}])}{\mathcal{T}(M^{\prime},[\omega^{\prime}])}\cdot\frac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})\\
a^{\prime}\in H_{1}(M^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\end{subarray}}{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\omega(a)-\pi{\rm i}\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}Z^{{\rm SO}(3)}_{a\oplus a^{\prime}}(M\#M^{\prime})}{\sum_{a^{\prime}\in H_{1}(M^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}Z^{{\rm SO}(3)}_{a^{\prime}}(M^{\prime})}\Bigg|_{q={\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

Assume that
b_{1}(M^{\prime})>0
and
\omega^{\prime}
is a generic element of
H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
. Then (see, e.g.,
[
74
]
)

\frac{\mathcal{T}(M\#M^{\prime},[\omega]\oplus[\omega^{\prime}])}{\mathcal{T}(M^{\prime},[\omega^{\prime}])}=|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|.

Taking a limit (possible since
b_{1}(M^{\prime})>0
) where
\omega^{\prime}
tends to an element of
H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
(e.g., zero) and using (
6.2
), we then have

\mathrm{N}_{r}^{0}(M,\omega)=|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|\frac{\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M\#M^{\prime},\omega\oplus\omega^{\prime})}{\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}.

Using
\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M\#M^{\prime},\omega\oplus\omega^{\prime})=D\cdot\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
,
we then indeed arrive at (
6.4
).

6.2
0-surgeries on twist knots

0-surgeries on knots,
M=S^{3}_{0}(K)
, all have
H_{1}(M)=\mathbb{Z}
. In such cases, the relation to the standard (not refined) WRT invariants is expected to be especially simple
[
20
]
:

\mathrm{WRT}_{r}(S^{3}_{0}(K))=-\frac{1}{2D^{2}}\big[\widehat{Z}_{0}^{(+)}+\widehat{Z}_{0}^{(-)}\big]\big|_{q\to{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}}.

This relation was explicitly verified in
[
20
]
for many twist knots and torus knots, and is expected to hold more generally. Here,

\widehat{Z}_{0}^{(+)}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=\mathrm{Res}_{x=0}\frac{x^{1/2}-x^{-1/2}}{x}F_{K}(x,q)=\frac{1}{2}f_{1}^{K}(q)

is
\widehat{Z}
-invariant in the trivial spin
c
structure. For twist knots, we also have

\widehat{Z}_{0}^{(-)}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=\mathrm{Res}_{x=x_{0}}\frac{x^{1/2}-x^{-1/2}}{x}F_{K}(x,q),

where
x_{0}
is the root of the Alexander polynomial with
\operatorname{Re}(x_{0})>0
. (Recall, that for a twist knot the Alexander polynomial has degree
2
, so that the corresponding residues differ by a sign.) Therefore, we can write

\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=-\frac{1}{2D^{2}}\lim_{q\to{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}}\oint_{C}\frac{{\rm d}x}{2\pi{\rm i}x}\frac{x^{1/2}-x^{-1/2}}{x}F_{K}(x,q)

where the contour
C
goes around
x=0
and
x_{0}
. It has been checked in
[
20
]
that this procedure indeed recovers the correct WRT invariants of
M=S^{3}_{0}(K)
for many twists knots
K
.

This way of recovering
\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)
is
a priori
different from the strategy used earlier in this paper, where the CGP and WRT invariants of
S^{3}_{0}(K)
are obtained from
F_{K}(x,q)
by first specializing
F_{K}(x,q)
to a root of unity
q={\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}
and then summing over colors / decorations
x
as in a typical surgery formula. Roughly speaking, this approach – based on the relation (
3.21
) to
\operatorname{ADO}_{r}
invariants – exchanges the order of operations, so that the limit
q\to{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}
comes first. It is instructive to verify that these two methods are compatible and, therefore, form a consistent network of proposed relations.
Namely, repeating the arguments of Appendix
B
for the
0
-surgeries on knots, we expect the following relation:

\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=-\frac{1}{2D^{2}}\lim_{q\to{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{Z}_{nk},

(6.5)

where, as in earlier sections, by
\widehat{Z}_{n}
we denote the invariant
\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{s}}(S^{3}(K))
associated to the spin
c
structure encoded by the integer
2n
on the knot (see Section
2.2
). Alternatively, according to (
3.21
),

\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=-\frac{1}{4D^{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{r-1}\big(\xi^{n}-\xi^{-n}\big)^{2}\operatorname{ADO}_{r}\big(\xi^{2n-2};K\big),

where we used
\Delta_{K}(1)=1
. Comparing the right-hand sides of the above two formulae we can eliminate
\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)
from these relations. Similar considerations apply to the invariants
\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)
; the only difference is that there is still
x
-dependence in all of the expressions, and, as explained in Section
3.7
, we obtain

\lim_{q\to{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}/r}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{Z}_{nr}x^{nr}=\sum_{n=0}^{r-1}\frac{\operatorname{ADO}_{r}\big(x\xi^{2n-2};K\big)}{2\Delta_{K}(x^{r})}\cdot\big(\xi^{n}x^{1/2}-\xi^{-n}x^{-1/2}\big)^{2}.

(6.6)

Since
\operatorname{ADO}_{r}(x;K)
is a polynomial in
x
, the right-hand side is expected to be a rational function in
x
. Let us illustrate how this works for the figure-8 knot. In the conventions (
3.30
), for the figure-8 knot we have
f_{1}=1
,
f_{3}=2
,
f_{5}=q^{-1}+3+q
, and so on. From (
3.31
), we get
\widehat{Z}_{0}=-2
,
\widehat{Z}_{1}=-1
,
\widehat{Z}_{2}=-q^{-1}-1-q
, etc. For example, for
r=2
and any knot
K
, with our conventions we have
\operatorname{ADO}_{2}(x;K)=\Delta_{K}(x)
and the right-hand side of the above relation becomes

\frac{\big(x-2+x^{-1}\big)\cdot\Delta_{K}(-x)+\big({-}x-2-x^{-1}\big)\cdot\Delta_{K}(x)}{2\Delta_{K}\big(x^{2}\big)}.

(6.7)

For example, for the figure-8 knot,
\Delta_{{\bf 4_{1}}}(x)=-x^{-1}+3-x
, and we get

-\frac{x^{2}-4+x^{-2}}{x^{2}-3+x^{-2}}=-1+x^{2}+3x^{4}+8x^{6}+21x^{8}+55x^{10}+144x^{12}+377x^{14}+987x^{16}+\cdots.

The coefficients of this expansion perfectly match
\widehat{Z}_{n}\big|_{q\to-1}
for even values of
n
.
21
21

21

Turning this around, we can say that
\big(x^{1/2}-x^{-1/2}\big)F_{K}(x,q)
restricted to even powers of
x
is a
q
-deformation of the rational function (
6.7
) determined by the Alexander polynomial.
Note, that (
6.6
) can be viewed as a close cousin of the Conjecture
1.1
, obtained from it by multiplying with
x^{\frac{1}{2}}-x^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, replacing
x
by
\xi^{2j}x
, and then summing over
j=0,\ldots,r-1
. This again verifies the consistency of various proposed relations.

In order to perform a similar computation for other values of
r
, it may be convenient, building on
[
33
,
55
]
, to express
\widehat{Z}_{n}(q)
in the quiver form,

\widehat{Z}_{n}(q)=2\tilde{f}_{n-1}(q)-\tilde{f}_{n-2}(q)-\tilde{f}_{n}(q),\qquad n>1,

where, for the figure-8 knot,

\tilde{f}_{n}(q)=\sum_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{6}=n}\big({-}q^{1/2}\big)^{n_{4}+n_{5}+n_{6}}q^{(n_{2}+n_{5})(n_{6}-n_{1})+\frac{1}{2}(n_{4}^{2}+n_{5}^{2}+n_{6}^{2})}\prod_{i=1}^{6}\frac{1}{(q;q)_{n_{i}}}.

Curiously, much like
f_{m}(q)
, the coefficients
\tilde{f}_{n}(q)
are all Laurent polynomials in
q
with integer coefficients.

Now, once we managed to write the right-hand side of (
6.5
) with the regularization parameter
x
as a rational function, it is straightforward to set
x=1
and obtain the WRT invariant. For example, for any knot
K
we have

\mathrm{WRT}_{2}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=1.

This is indeed what we expect from the surgery formula for

\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)=D^{-2}\sum_{n=1}^{r-1}(-1)^{n+1}[n]J_{n-1}(q)\Big|_{q={\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

6.3
From CGP to WRT via
\bm{\widehat{Z}}
for 0-surgeries on knots

Consider the general surgery formula (
2.33
) for
\widehat{Z}
in the case of 0-surgery on a knot
K
. We have
V=b_{1}=1
,
\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}=1
,
\sigma=0
,
B=0
and

\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(b^{\prime\prime},s)}=q^{b^{\prime\prime}}F_{2b^{\prime\prime}},

(6.8)

where
b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\cong H_{1}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K);\mathbb{Z}\big)
runs over integers and
F_{2b^{\prime\prime}}
are the coefficients of the following formal power series:

F(x,q):=F_{K}\big(x^{2},q\big)\big(x-x^{-1}\big)=\sum_{\ell}F_{\ell}x^{\ell}.

For knots only even powers actually appear in the series
F(x,q)
(not to be confused with
F_{K}(x,q)
).

6.3.1
Odd level
\bm{r}

As before, denote
\mu:=\omega(\mathfrak{m})\in\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
, where
\mathfrak{m}
is the class of the meridian of the know in
H_{1}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K);\mathbb{Z}\big)
. The formula (
3.15
) in the case of a 0-surgery on
K
then reads

\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=r\frac{\Delta\big({\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\mu}\big)}{\big({\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\mu}-{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\mu}\big)^{2}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\mu m}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(rm,s)}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

Plugging in (
6.8
) and using
\Delta(1)=1
, we then have

\lim_{\mu\rightarrow 0}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=\lim_{\mu\rightarrow 1}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=-\frac{D^{2}}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F_{2mr}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

(6.9)

On the other hand, for WRT invariant we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big)

\displaystyle=D^{-2}\sum_{n=1}^{r-1}(-1)^{n+1}J_{n-1}(q)[n]=-\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}}F\big(q^{n/2},q\big)|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{Z}_{r}\\
b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}nb^{\prime\prime}}{r}}F_{2b^{\prime\prime}}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F_{2mr}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

(6.10)

Combining (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) we get

\lim_{\mu\rightarrow 0}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=\lim_{\mu\rightarrow 1}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=D^{2}\operatorname{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K)\big),

which is in agreement with Theorem
2.12
.

6.3.2
Even level
\bm{r}

The formula (
3.14
) in the case of a 0-surgery on
K
then reads

\mathrm{N}_{r}(M,\omega)=r\frac{\Delta_{K}\big({\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\mu}\big)}{\big({\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\mu}-{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\mu}\big)^{2}}\sum_{m}{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\mu m}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(\frac{rm}{2},s)}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

Plugging in (
6.8
) we then have

\lim_{\mu\rightarrow 0}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=-\frac{D^{2}}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{m}F_{mr}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}},

(6.11)

and

\lim_{\mu\rightarrow 1}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=-\frac{D^{2}}{2}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}F_{mr}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

(6.12)

On the other hand, for
r=2\bmod 4
one can consider WRT invariant refined by an element
\gamma\in H^{1}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K);\mathbb{Z}_{2}\big)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}

[
50
]
. Let
c=\gamma(\mathfrak{m})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{WRT}_{r}(S^{3}_{0}(K),\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=D^{-2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=0\\
n=c+1\bmod 2\end{subarray}}^{r-1}(-1)^{n+1}J_{n-1}(q)[n]=D^{-2}\sum_{n\in 2\mathbb{Z}_{r/2}+c+1}F\big(q^{n/2}\big)\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=-\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathbb{Z}_{r/2}\\
b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}(2a+c+1)b^{\prime\prime}}{r}}F_{2b^{\prime\prime}}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{m(c+1)}F_{mr}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

(6.13)

Combining (
6.11
), (
6.12
) and (
6.13
), we get

\lim_{\mu\rightarrow c}[r\mu]^{2}\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\omega\big)=2D^{2}\operatorname{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}_{0}(K),\gamma\big),\qquad c=0,1,

which is again consistent with Theorem
2.12
.

Appendix A
Spin and spin
c
sign-refined torsion

The Reidemeister torsion has an intrinsic sign ambiguity. As was shown by Turaev, it is possible to fix it by choosing an Euler structure
[
72
]
. In the case of 3-manifolds, such choice is equivalent to a choice of a spin
c
structure
[
73
]
. Consider a 3-manifold
M=S^{3}(\mathcal{L})
obtained by a surgery on a framed link
\mathcal{L}\in S^{3}
. As before, let
\mathrm{Vert}
be the set of components of the link and
B_{IJ}
,
I,J\in\mathrm{Vert}
its linking matrix. Denote by
\sigma_{K}(M)\in\mathrm{Spin}^{c}
a spin
c
structure that corresponds to a characteristic vector
K\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/2B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
,
K_{I}=B_{II}\bmod 2
, as in (
2.18
). Let
a\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
and
\alpha_{I}:=a(\mathfrak{m}_{I})\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
. They satisfy the condition
\sum_{J}B_{IJ}\alpha_{J}=0\bmod 1
. As before, denote
\varepsilon=(1,1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
. The Turaev’s sign refined torsion of
M=S^{3}(\mathcal{L})
is then given by the following formula
[
74
]
(see also
[
12
]
):

\mathcal{T}(M,a,\sigma_{K})=(-1)^{b_{+}}\prod_{I}\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}-{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}}\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\big(\big\{{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}\big\}_{I}\big){\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}(-\alpha^{T}K+\varepsilon^{T}B\alpha)},

where
\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}
is the Alexander–Conway function of the link
\mathcal{L}
.

The above torsion relates to the invariant
\mathrm{N}_{2}(M,\omega)
, where
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})\setminus H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
.
Indeed, in
[
24
]
(Theorem 6.23, taking into account the different normalisation used in the present paper) the following was proved:

\mathcal{T}\bigg(M,\frac{\omega}{2},\sigma_{K}\bigg)=(-2)^{1+b_{1}(M)}\bigg(\frac{\rm i}{4}\bigg)^{b_{1}(M)}\frac{{\rm i}\mathrm{N}_{2}(M,\omega)}{2}{\rm i}^{-\frac{\mu^{T}B\mu}{2}-K^{T}\mu},

where we used that
\frac{\omega}{2}\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z})
is the well defined cohomology class the value of which on the meridian
\mathfrak{m}_{I}
is
\frac{\omega(\mathfrak{m}_{I})}{2}=\frac{\mu_{I}}{2}
and we encoded the spin
c
structure
\sigma_{K}
via
K
as above.

In particular, using the canonical map
i\colon\mathrm{Spin}(M)\to\mathrm{Spin}^{c}(M)
we can define an invariant which depends only on a spin structure
s
as

\mathcal{T}_{s}\bigg(M,\frac{\omega}{2}\bigg):=\mathcal{T}\bigg(M,\frac{\omega}{2},i(s)\bigg).

More explicitly, if we now let
\tilde{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
be such that
\tilde{s}=s\bmod 2
and
K=\sum_{I,J\in\mathrm{Vert}}B_{IJ}\tilde{s}_{J}
then it holds:

\mathcal{T}_{s}\bigg(M,\frac{\omega}{2}\bigg):=(-2)^{1+b_{1}}\bigg(\frac{\rm i}{4}\bigg)^{b_{1}(M)}\frac{{\rm i}\mathrm{N}_{2}(M,\omega)}{2}{\rm i}^{-\frac{\mu^{T}B\mu}{2}-\tilde{s}B\mu}.

It is possible to define the version of the torsion depending only on spin structure (cf.
[
59
]
). Using the map (
3.11
), consider

\mathcal{T}_{s}(M,a):=\mathcal{T}(M,a,\sigma(b,s)){\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}a(b)}.

From the surgery formula, it is easy to see that the right-hand side depends only on spin structure
s\in\mathrm{Spin}(M)
, but not
b\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})
. Namely, using the correspondence (
2.5
), we have
K=2b+Bs
for
\sigma_{K}=\sigma(b,s)
, where
b\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
,
s\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/2\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
,
\sum_{J}B_{IJ}s_{J}=B_{II}\bmod 2
. Therefore, for
M=S^{3}(\mathcal{L})
we have

\mathcal{T}_{s}(M,a)=(-1)^{b_{+}}\prod_{I}\frac{1}{{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}-{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}}\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}\big(\big\{{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}\big\}_{I}\big){\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}(\varepsilon-s)^{T}B\alpha}.

In particular, for a plumbed
M
we have

\mathcal{T}_{s}(M,a)=(-1)^{b_{+}}\prod_{I}\big({\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}-{\rm e}^{-\pi{\rm i}\alpha_{I}}\big)^{\deg(I)-2}{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}(\varepsilon-s)^{T}B\alpha}.

(A.1)

Note that if
a=\omega\bmod H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, where
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
the dependence on the spin structure disappears, as
B\alpha=B\omega\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
.

Appendix B
\bm{H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})}
-refined WRT invariant and
\bm{\widehat{Z}}

In
[
25
]
, building on
[
10
,
50
]
the authors define a refined Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant
\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)
for
r\neq 0\bmod 4
that depends on a choice of
\omega\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
, see Definition
2.6
. In this appendix we relate this invariant to
\widehat{Z}
.
Let
M=S^{3}(L)
be the 3-manifold obtained by a surgery on a framed link
L
. We will use the same conventions as before. Let
J_{n-\varepsilon}(L)\in\mathbb{Z}\big[q^{\pm 1/4}\big]
be the Jones polynomial of
L
colored by
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
representations of dimensions
n\in\{1,2,3,\ldots\}^{\mathrm{Vert}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}\big(S^{3}(\mathcal{L}),\omega\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{D^{-b_{0}-b_{1}}}{(\Delta_{+}^{{\rm SO}(3)})^{b_{+}}(\Delta_{-}^{{\rm SO}(3)})^{b_{-}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\{1,2,\ldots,r-1\}^{\mathrm{Vert}}\\
n=\mu+\varepsilon\bmod 2\end{subarray}}J_{n-1}[\mathcal{L}]\prod_{I\in\mathrm{Vert}}(-1)^{n_{I}+1}\frac{q^{n_{I}/2}-q^{-n_{I}/2}}{q^{1/2}-q^{-1/2}}\bigg|_{q={\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}},

where as before
\mu_{I}:=\omega_{I}(\mathfrak{m}_{I})
. For
r=2\bmod 4
, this invariant is a slight modification of the invariant of
[
51
]
(see also
[
10
]
). For odd
r
, the invariant satisfies

\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,0)=\operatorname{WRT}_{r}^{{\rm SO}(3)}(M),

where
\operatorname{WRT}_{r}^{{\rm SO}(3)}(M)
is the
\operatorname{SO}(3)
version of the WRT invariant introduced in
[
51
]
.

To conjecture a relationship between
\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)
and
\widehat{Z}_{\mathfrak{s}}(M)
consider again the case of plumbing surgery. In this case,

\displaystyle J[\mathcal{L}]_{n-\varepsilon}={}

\displaystyle\frac{q^{\frac{\sum_{I}B_{II}(n_{I}^{2}-1)}{4}}}{q^{1/2}-q^{-1/2}}\prod_{I\in\mathrm{Vert}}(-1)^{(n_{I}+1)(B_{II}+1)}\big({q^{n_{I}/2}-q^{-n_{I}/2}}\big)^{1-\text{deg}(I)}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{(I,J)\in\text{Edges}}\big(q^{n_{I}n_{J}/2}-q^{-n_{I}n_{J}/2}\big).

We proceed similarly to Section
3.1

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=\frac{(-1)^{b_{-}}r^{-V/2}\xi^{\frac{3\sigma-\operatorname{Tr}B}{2}}}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}}\cdot\begin{cases}{\rm e}^{\frac{\pi{\rm i}\sigma}{2}}&\text{if}\quad r=1\bmod 4,\\
2^{-V/2}{\rm e}^{\frac{3\pi{\rm i}\sigma}{4}}&\text{if}\quad r=2\bmod 4,\\
{\rm e}^{\pi{\rm i}\sigma}&\text{if}\quad r=3\bmod 4,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}=\sum_{\ell}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}F_{\ell},\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tilde{n}\in(\mathbb{Z}/2r\mathbb{Z})^{\mathrm{Vert}}\\
\tilde{n}=\mu+(r-1)\varepsilon\bmod 2\end{subarray}}\xi^{\ell^{T}\tilde{n}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{n}^{T}B\tilde{n}}.

We have used the following fact:

(-1)^{\sum_{I}(n_{I}+1)B_{II}}\big|_{n=\mu+\varepsilon\bmod 2}=(-1)^{\sum_{I}\mu_{I}B_{II}}=(-1)^{\mu^{T}B\mu}=1

since
B\mu=0\bmod 2
,
where
F_{\ell}
are the same as in Section
3.1
.
Applying Gauss reciprocity we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}=\xi^{-\frac{\ell^{T}B^{-1}\ell}{2}}\frac{{\rm e}^{\frac{\pi{\rm i}\sigma}{4}}(r/2)^{V/2}}{|\det B|^{1/2}}\underbrace{\sum_{\tilde{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/2B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}\tilde{a}^{T}B^{-1}\tilde{a}-\pi{\rm i}\tilde{a}^{T}B^{-1}(\ell+B(\mu+\varepsilon))}}_{=:\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}^{\prime}=\sum_{{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\sum_{A\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/2\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\!\!\!{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}{a}^{T}B^{-1}{a}-\pi{\rm i}rA^{T}a-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}A^{T}BA-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}(s+\mu)-\pi{\rm i}A^{T}Bs}.

B.1
Level
\bm{r=2\bmod 4}

The sum in Appendix
B
simplifies to

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}^{\prime}=2^{V}\sum_{{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\exp\left\{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}{a}^{T}B^{-1}{a}-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}(s+\mu)\right\}.

(B.1)

Combining everything together, we then have

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)={}

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{b_{+}}}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}|\det B|^{1/2}}\xi^{\frac{3\sigma-\operatorname{Tr}B}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\sum_{{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}F_{\ell}\xi^{-\frac{\ell^{T}B^{-1}\ell}{2}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}{a}^{T}B^{-1}{a}-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}(s+\mu)}.

We can then conjecture the following general relation for a rational homology
M
and
r=2\allowbreak\bmod 4
:

\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)=\frac{1}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}}\sum_{a,b\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}r}{2}q_{s}(a)-2\pi{\rm i}{\ell k}(a,b)-\pi{\rm i}\omega(a)}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(b,s)}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

B.2
Level
\bm{r=1\bmod 4}

Applying a version of the Gauss reciprocity formula to the sum over
A
in Appendix
B
, we can rewrite it as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}^{\prime}={}\frac{{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}\sigma}{4}}2^{V/2}}{|\det B|^{1/2}}\sum_{a,f\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\exp

\displaystyle\biggl\{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}(r-1)}{2}a^{T}B^{-1}a-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}\mu

\displaystyle\hphantom{\biggl\lx@text@lbrace}+2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}f+2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}a+\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{2}s^{T}Bs\biggr\}.

Combining everything together, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)={}

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{b_{+}}}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}|\det B|}{\rm e}^{\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{2}(s^{T}Bs-\sigma)}\xi^{\frac{3\sigma-\operatorname{Tr}B}{2}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\sum_{a,f\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}F_{\ell}\xi^{-\frac{\ell^{T}B^{-1}\ell}{2}}

\displaystyle\times{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}(r-1)}{2}a^{T}B^{-1}a-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}\mu+2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}f+2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}a}.

Taking into account (
2.17
), we can then conjecture the following general relation for a rational homology
M
and
r=1\bmod 4
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{2}\mu(M,s)}}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}\sum_{a,b,f\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\left(-\frac{r-1}{4}{\ell k}(a,a)+{\ell k}(a,f-b)-\frac{1}{2}\omega(a)+{\ell k}(f,f)\right)}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(b,s)}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

B.3
Level
\bm{r=3\bmod 4}

This case is analogous to the case
r=1\bmod 4
considered above. Applying a version of the Gauss reciprocity formula to the sum over
A
in Appendix
B
, we can rewrite it as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}^{\prime}=\frac{{\rm e}^{\frac{\pi{\rm i}\sigma}{4}}2^{V/2}}{|\det B|^{1/2}}\sum_{a,f\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\exp

\displaystyle\biggl\{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}(r+1)}{2}a^{T}B^{-1}a-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}\mu

\displaystyle\hphantom{\biggl\lx@text@lbrace}-2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}f-2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}a-\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{2}s^{T}Bs\biggr\}.

Combining everything together we then have

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)={}

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{b_{+}}}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}|\det B|}{\rm e}^{\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{2}\left(\sigma-s^{T}Bs\right)}\xi^{\frac{3\sigma-\operatorname{Tr}B}{2}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}\sum_{a,f\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}/B\mathbb{Z}^{\mathrm{Vert}}}F_{\ell}\xi^{-\frac{\ell^{T}B^{-1}\ell}{2}}

\displaystyle\times{\rm e}^{-\frac{\pi{\rm i}(r-1)}{2}a^{T}B^{-1}a-2\pi{\rm i}a^{T}B^{-1}b-\pi{\rm i}a^{T}\mu+2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}f+2\pi{\rm i}f^{T}B^{-1}a}.

We can then conjecture the following general relation for a rational homology
M
and
r=3

\bmod~4
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{WRT}_{r}(M,\omega)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\frac{{\rm e}^{\frac{\pi{\rm i}}{2}\mu(M,s)}}{{\rm i}\sqrt{8r}|H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})|}\sum_{a,b,f\in H_{1}(M;\mathbb{Z})}{\rm e}^{2\pi{\rm i}\left(-\frac{r+1}{4}{\ell k}(a,a)-{\ell k}(a,f+b)-\frac{1}{2}\omega(a)-{\ell k}(f,f)\right)}\widehat{Z}_{\sigma(b,s)}\Big|_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}.

Appendix C
Graded bases of the CGP TQFT

In
[
12
]
, a TQFT
\mathbb{V}
was built by applying the universal construction to the invariants
\mathrm{N}_{r}
.
It turns out that the functor one gets is a symmetric monoidal one from a suitable category of decorated cobordisms into that of graded vector spaces, endowed with the symmetry which is the flip if
r
is odd and is the supersymmetric exchange if
r
is even.

The cobordisms considered for this construction are
3
-manifolds
M
with boundary endowed with cohomology classes
\omega\in H^{1}(M,\{*\};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
where
\{*\}
is the choice of a base point per each connected component of the boundary (besides other standard decorations). Furthermore, by definition

\mathbb{V}_{k}(\Sigma,\omega)=V\big((\Sigma,\omega)\sqcup\big(S^{2},V_{0}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\sigma^{\otimes k}\big)\big),

where
\sigma
is the
1
-dimensional module over
U^{H}_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
with weight
2r^{\prime}
and
V
is the vector space associated to a surface by applying directly the universal construction. Finally, one lets
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)=\bigoplus_{k}\mathbb{V}_{k}(\Sigma,\omega)
which is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, but it turns out that it is always finite dimensional.
As proved in
[
12
]
Theorem 5.9, letting

\dim_{t}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma))=\begin{cases}\sum t^{k}\dim(\mathbb{V}_{k}(\Sigma))&\text{if\quad$r$ is odd,}\\
\sum(-t)^{k}\dim(\mathbb{V}_{k}(\Sigma))&\text{if\quad$r$ is even},\end{cases}

then if
\omega\in H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})\setminus H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
then it holds

\dim_{t^{-2r^{\prime}}}(\mathbb{V}(\Sigma_{g}))=\frac{(r^{\prime})^{g}}{r}\sum_{k\in H_{r}}\bigg(\frac{t^{r}-t^{-r}}{t\xi^{k}-t^{-1}\xi^{-k}}\bigg)^{2g-2}.

In particular, the ungraded dimension for
g\geq 2
is
r^{3g-3}
if
r
is odd and
\frac{r^{3g-3}}{2^{g-1}}
if
r
is even.

Remark C.1
.

The value of the Verlinde formula coincides with the value of the invariant
Z_{r}
which is NOT equal to
\mathrm{N}_{r}
:

Z_{r}=\bigg(\frac{(-1)^{r-1}}{\sqrt{r^{\prime}}}\bigg)^{b_{0}}\bigg(\frac{(-1)^{r-1}\sqrt{r^{\prime}}}{r}\bigg)^{b_{1}}\mathrm{N}_{r}.

Example C.2
.

If
g=3
the graded dimensions of
\dim_{t^{-2r^{\prime}}}\mathbb{V}(\Sigma)
are

\displaystyle r=2\colon\ \dim_{t^{-2r^{\prime}}}\mathbb{V}(\Sigma)=t^{4}-4t^{2}+6-4t^{-2}+t^{-4},

\displaystyle r=3\colon\ \dim_{t^{-2r^{\prime}}}\mathbb{V}(\Sigma)=108t^{6}+513+108t^{-6},

\displaystyle r=4\colon\ \dim_{t^{-2r^{\prime}}}\mathbb{V}(\Sigma)=-8t^{12}+80t^{8}-248t^{4}+352-248t^{-4}+80t^{8}-8t^{-12}.

Example C.3
.

The above Verlinde formula applies only when
(\Sigma,\omega)
is admissible.
For instance, when
\Sigma=S^{2}
this is not the case and indeed the invariant is not a Laurent polynomial:

\mathrm{N}_{r}\big(S^{2}\times S^{1},\beta\big)=\sum_{k\in H_{r}}\frac{\big(q^{\beta+k}-q^{-\beta-k}\big)^{2}}{\big(q^{r\beta}-q^{-r\beta}\big)^{2}}=\frac{2r}{\big(q^{r\beta}-q^{-r\beta}\big)^{2}},

where
\beta\in H^{1}\big(S^{2}\times S^{1};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}\big)
is a cohomology class the value of which on
\{pt\}\times S^{1}
is
\beta\in\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
.

Example C.4
.

A special case is when
r=2
.
In this case, if
(\Sigma,\omega)
is admissible one gets

\dim_{t^{-2}}\mathbb{V}(\Sigma)=\big(t-t^{-1}\big)^{2g-2}{\rm i}^{2-2g}.

Then applying
[
12
, Proposition 6.22]
, one recovers the Conway polynomial of the link in Figure
4
:

\nabla_{L}=\big(q^{\alpha_{0}}-q^{-\alpha_{0}}\big)^{2g-1}\prod_{i=1}^{2g}\big(q^{\alpha_{i}}-q^{-\alpha_{i}}\big),

where
\alpha_{0}
is the color of the main strand and
\alpha_{i}
are the colors of the remaining
2g
ones.

Figure 4:
The link surgering on which provides
\Sigma_{g}\times S^{1}
.

The definition of the
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)
can be given in a more intrinsic way as follows.
Let
Y
be a three-manifold obtained by taking the complement of an open ball in a three-dimensional handlebody, so that
\partial Y=\Sigma\sqcup S^{2}
.
As detailed in
[
12
]
,
\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)
is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded with the grading being induced by the action of
H^{0}(\Sigma)
as follows: if
\phi\in H^{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
we can map it to a cohomology class in
H^{0}(\{*\};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
(where
\{*\}
is set formed by base point on
\Sigma
and one on
S^{2}
) by extending it to
0
on
S^{2}
; then let
\delta(\phi)\in H^{1}(Y,\{*\};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
be the cohomology class induced by the exact sequence of the pair
(Y,\{*\})
. Observe that its restriction to
(\partial Y\setminus\{*\})
is the zero cohomology class so that if
W\in H^{1}(Y,\{*\};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
is a class the restriction of which to
H^{1}(\Sigma,\{*\};\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
is
\omega
then also
W+\delta(\phi)
is. We say that a vector
[Y,W]\in\mathbb{V}(\Sigma,\omega)
is of degree
k
if for each
\phi\in H^{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z})
we have
[Y,\omega+\delta\phi]=[Y,\omega]q^{2r^{\prime}k\phi}
. It turns out that only some integer values of
k
are possible.

For generic
\omega
, a basis of
\mathbb{V}_{0}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
is obtained as follows. Let
\Gamma
be an oriented trivalent graph the thickening of which is a handlebody
H_{g}
of genus
g
the boundary of which is identified with
\Sigma
. An edge
e
of
\Gamma
is colored by
\overline{\alpha}(e):=\omega(m_{e})\in\mathbb{C}/2\mathbb{Z}
where
m_{e}
is the oriented meridian of the edge. Then consider a lift
\alpha\colon\operatorname{Edges}(\Gamma)\to\mathbb{C}
of
\overline{\alpha}
such that
(\partial\alpha)(v)\in H_{r}
for every vertex
v\in\Gamma
. Furthermore, restrict to those
\alpha
such that the real part of
\alpha(e)
is between
[0,2r[
for a fixed arbitrary edge
e
.
Such a set is a basis of
\mathbb{V}_{0}(\Sigma_{g},\omega)
.

Appendix D
Commutativity of limits

In this section, we show that the assumption (i) of the Theorem
4.18
is satisfied for a certain subclass of plumbing links. We will need the following proposition, which is slight generalization of a corollary to a proposition in
[
58
]
.
22
22

22

The proposition in
[
58
]
considers a slightly less general regularization.

Proposition D.1
.

Let
C\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be a function with a period
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and mean value 0. Then

(i)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\sum_{n\geq 1}C(n){\rm e}^{-\epsilon(n+\gamma)}=-\sum_{n=1}^{M}\frac{n}{M}C(n)
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\sum_{n\geq 1}C(n){\rm e}^{-\epsilon(n^{2}+2\alpha n+\beta)}=-\sum_{n=1}^{M}\frac{n}{M}C(n)

for any
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
.

Proof.

Can be given for example using Euler–Maclaren asymptotic summation formula:

\sum_{n\geq 0}f(n)=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)\,{\rm d}x-\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{B_{r}^{-}}{r!}f^{(r-1)}(0).

Only the integral part and the term
r=1
in the sum will contribute to constant and possibly singular terms of the expansion in
\epsilon
. The singular terms and the constant terms depending on
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
will cancel out due to the zero mean value condition.
∎

Consider a plumbing tree with vertex
I=0
of valency 3, three vertices of valency one (
I=1,2,3
) and possibly other vertices of valency two. We then have

F_{L}\big(x^{2};q\big)\prod_{I}\big(x_{I}-x_{I}^{-1}\big)=\big(x_{1}-x_{1}^{-1}\big)\big(x_{2}-x_{2}^{-1}\big)\big(x_{3}-x_{3}^{-1}\big)\sum_{n\geq 1}\big(x_{0}^{2n-1}-x_{0}^{-2n+1}\big).

(D.1)

In the case
\det B\neq 0
(i.e.,
b_{1}=0
), after
t
-regularization and the Laplace transform the sum over
n
above will take the following form (up to a finite number of terms, which do not affect the issue of commutativity of the limits):

\mathfrak{L}_{\omega}F_{L}\big(x^{2};q\big)\prod_{I}\big(x_{I}-x_{I}^{-1}\big)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}C_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(n)q^{\frac{-B^{-1}_{00}}{4}(n^{2}+2\alpha n+\beta)}t^{2n+\gamma},

(D.2)

where the sum over
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
is over a finite set of rational numbers and
C_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(n)
are periodic in
n
. For the sum to give a well-defined element in
\mathbb{C}\big(\big(q^{1/p}\big)\big)
, we require
B_{00}^{-1}<0
. The zero mean value condition is satisfied because of the alternating signs in the expansion of (
D.1
). We then have

\lim_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\mathfrak{L}_{\omega}F_{L}\big(x^{2};q\big)\prod_{I}\big(x_{I}-x_{I}^{-1}\big)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\tilde{C}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(n){\rm e}^{-\epsilon(n^{2}+2\alpha n+\beta)}

and

\lim_{t\rightarrow 1}\lim_{q\rightarrow{\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{r}}}\mathfrak{L}_{\omega}F_{L}\big(x^{2};q\big)\prod_{I}\big(x_{I}-x_{I}^{-1}\big)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0+}\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\tilde{C}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(n){\rm e}^{-\epsilon(n+\gamma)},

where

\tilde{C}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(n):=C_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(n){\rm e}^{\frac{-2\pi{\rm i}B^{-1}_{00}}{4r}(n^{2}+2\alpha n+\beta)}

are also periodic (generally with a larger period). Using the Proposition
D.1
, we can then check commutativity of the limits.

The analysis can be extended to the case
b_{1}>0
. This in particular covers the case of 0-surgeries on torus knots, which can be related to the plumbings of this type by Kirby moves. The main modification is that in (
D.2
) one has to replace
B_{00}^{-1}
with
\sum_{i,j=1}^{V-b_{1}}U_{i0}U_{j0}(B^{\prime})^{-1}_{ij}
(see Section
2.6
for the notation), which is again required to be negative in order for
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
operation to be well-defined.
